
[PDF PAGE 1]
Statistical Inference for Low-Rank Tensor Models
Ke Xu∗1, Elynn Chen†2, and Yuefeng Han‡1
1Department of Applied and Computational Mathematics and Statistics, University of
Notre Dame
2Department of Technology, Operations, and Statistics, New York University
Abstract
Statistical inference for tensors has emerged as a critical challenge in analyzing high-dimensional
data in modern data science. This paper introduces a unified framework for inferring general and
low-Tucker-rank linear functionals of low-Tucker-rank signal tensors for several low-rank tensor mod-
els. Our methodology tackles two primary goals: achieving asymptotic normality and constructing
minimax-optimal confidence intervals. By leveraging a debiasing strategy and projecting onto the
tangent space of the low-Tucker-rank manifold, we enable inference for general and structured lin-
ear functionals, extending far beyond the scope of traditional entrywise inference. Specifically, in
the low-Tucker-rank tensor regression or PCA model, we establish the computational and statistical
efficiency of our approach, achieving near-optimal sample size requirements (in regression model)
and signal-to-noise ratio (SNR) conditions (in PCA model) for general linear functionals without
requiring sparsity in the loading tensor. Our framework also attains both computationally and sta-
tistically optimal sample size and SNR thresholds for low-Tucker-rank linear functionals. Numerical
experiments validate our theoretical results, showcasing the framework’s utility in diverse applica-
tions. This work addresses significant methodological gaps in statistical inference, advancing tensor
analysis for complex and high-dimensional data environments.
Keywords: asymptotic normality, statistical inference, Tucker decomposition, Principal Component
Analysis, tensor regression.
1 Introduction
In recent years, the study of tensors and high-dimensional arrays has gained significant attention across
fields such as statistics, applied mathematics, machine learning, and data science. Tensors frequently
appear in scientific domains such as compressed sensing [6, 18], neuroimaging [27, 3], recommendation
systems [19, 55], and econometrics and finance [26, 44]. These tensors are often high-dimensional, with
their ambient dimensions far exceeding the sample size. Yet, many practical scenarios reveal that tensors
possess low-dimensional structures, such as low rank or sparsity [23, 40], driving advancements in tensor
estimation and structural recovery techniques.
Despite substantial progress in tensor estimation [9, 22, 24, 51, 46], statistical inference for tensors
remains relatively underexplored. Most existing studies focus on matrix/tensor completion and recovery
from missing data [11, 32, 45], with less attention paid to fully observed tensor data. Furthermore, much
of the existing work has emphasized entrywise inference [11, 1]. In contrast, the inference of general
linear functionals—expressed as ⟨T , A⟩, where A is a loading tensor and T ∈Rp1×p2×p3 represents the
signal tensor of interest—has been largely overlooked. These linear functionals are crucial in various
applications, offering flexibility in capturing both localized and aggregated features. To demonstrate
this, we present three example tasks.
Task 1: Inference of Specific Entries. In many real-world applications, researchers often focus on in-
ferring specific entries of the signal tensor T . In these cases, the loading tensor A is typically sparse,
containing only a few non-zero elements corresponding to the targeted entries. For example, tensor-based
∗kxu6@nd.edu
†elynn.chen@stern.nyu.edu
‡yuefeng.han@nd.edu

arXiv:2501.16223v1  [math.ST]  27 Jan 2025

methods have been used to analyze spatiotemporal gene expression data, capturing interactions among
genes, regions, and time points using a three-mode gene expression tensor [29]. If the goal is to compare
the expression levels of gene g in the region s at two different time points t1 and t2, the difference of
interest can be expressed as ⟨T , A⟩ = Tg,s,t1 − Tg,s,t2 .
Task 2: Inference of a Subgroup of Entries. In some scenarios, the linear functional ⟨T , A⟩ involves
a subgroup of entries across one or more modes of the signal tensor T . For instance, in recommender
systems, the (i, j, k)-th entry of a third-order tensor may represent interactions among user i, item j, and
context k. To decide whether to recommend item either j1 or j2 to a group of users, indexed by G ⊆[p1],
this decision-making can be formalized by testing the condition⟨T , A⟩ = P
i∈G⟨ei⊗(ej1 −ej2 )⊤⊗ek, T ⟩>
0, as demonstrated in Zhang et al. [55], Xia and Yuan [47]. Similarly, in network traffic analysis, traffic
data indexed by source s, destination g, and time t can be represented as a tensor [57]. The total traffic
at time t is captured by the linear functional ⟨T , A⟩ = P
s,g Ts,g,t, which aggregates entries across specific
modes.
Task 3: Inference of General Linear Functionals. In more general cases, the loading tensor A exhibits a
more complex structure. For example, in neuroimaging studies, the relationship between brain imaging
data and continuous clinical or cognitive assessment scores is modeled using a parameter tensor T [28].
In this context, given an input image A, the fitted clinical score is ⟨T , A⟩. Unlike simpler cases, the input
image A is not necessarily of low Tucker rank. Inference on this general linear functional is essential to
quantify the uncertainty of the predicted clinical outcomes.
These examples underscore the need for statistical inference methods capable of handling linear func-
tionals of signal tensors, with loading tensors A exhibiting diverse sparsity and structural complexity.
While entrywise inference (Task 1) has been studied in tensor completion, Tasks 2 and 3 involve linear
functionals with potentially many nonzero entries and more complex structures, which are largely un-
derexplored. To accommodate this diversity, we propose to model the complexity of the loading tensor
through its Tucker rank. For entrywise inference (Task 1), the Tucker rank ofA is near minimal, typically
rank(A) ≈ (1, 1, 1). For subgroup aggregate inference (Task 2), the Tucker rank may range from (1 , 1, 1)
to a small tuple, such as ( R1, R2, R3). In more general applications (Task 3), A may reach full rank,
i.e., (p1, p2, p3). Using Tucker rank to model A’s complexity provides a unified framework for handling
different structural requirements.
Tensor regression and tensor principal component analysis (PCA) are two fundamental problems
that motivate the exploration of signal tensors. Both approaches leverage low-rank tensor structures
to address the challenges of high-dimensional data. For a comprehensive discussion on low-rank tensor
models, we refer readers to Bi et al. [3], Liu et al. [30]. This naturally raises a critical question:
• What are the sample size or signal-to-noise ratio (SNR) requirements for inferring
general linear functionals of the parameters in low-rank tensor models?
The field of tensor inference builds on advancements in statistical inference for low-rank matrices,
as matrices can be seen as mode-2 tensors. Cai et al. [5] investigated the inference of general linear
functionals under the low-rank matrix trace regression framework with Gaussian design. However, their
results required a sample size proportional to p1p2 to construct a valid confidence interval for a low-rank
parameter matrix M ∈ Rp1×p2 with rank r. This prompts the question: can valid inferences for general
linear functionals be achieved with fewer samples than the order of p1p2p3 for a low-rank signal tensor
T ∈Rp1×p2×p3 in tensor regression?
In this paper, we focus on statistical inference for both full-Tucker-rank (referred to as general
linear functionals throughout this article) and low-T ucker-rank linear functionalsof low-Tucker-
rank signal tensors. Our investigation centers on two fundamental settings: tensor regression and tensor
PCA. In both cases, the least squares estimator in tensor regression and the estimator in tensor PCA
can be viewed as the true signal tensor corrupted by either complex or simple noise terms. We develop
a unified inference framework that debiases the initial estimate and projects it onto the singular spaces
of the signal tensor. This framework constructs confidence intervals by identifying the pivot quantity
on the tangent space of the low-Tucker-rank manifold. It accommodates a wide variety of loading
tensor structures, extending beyond entrywise inference to facilitate applications involving general linear
functionals of the signal tensor. With certain modifications, our framework could extend beyond tensor
regression and PCA to accommodate a range of low-rank tensor models, including tensor completion
[51, 48], generalized tensor estimation [22], and high-order tensor clustering [21, 31], enabling inference
for diverse low-rank structures.

between the incoherence and alignment conditions is presented in Section 3.6.
Inference for General Linear F unctionals We establish central limit theorems that facilitate the
statistical inference for general linear functionals in both tensor regression and PCA frameworks. To the
best of our knowledge, this is the first work to address the inference of general linear functionals without
imposing incoherence conditions on the loading tensor A in tensor models, filling a significant gap in the
literature. Previous studies have primarily concentrated on tensor completion, recovery from missing
data, and entrywise inference, leaving more complex inference problems underexplored. We introduce
a unified framework for inferring general linear functionals of signal tensors, thereby expanding the
applicability of tensor analysis to a broader range of practical scenarios. While our focus is on third-
order tensors for clarity, the framework is readily extensible to higher-order tensors.
Our results achieve nearly computationally optimal sample sizes in tensor regression and optimal
signal-to-noise ratio (SNR) requirements in tensor PCA, all without additional incoherence conditions.
Specifically, for a signal tensor T ∈Rp1×p2×p3 with p = maxj=1,2,3 pj, we demonstrate that a sample size
of n ≳ p2r suffices to infer any linear functional in tensor regression. This improves upon the matrix trace
regression results by Cai et al. [5], which require n ≳ p1p2 for a low-rank parameter matrix M ∈ Rp1×p2 .
For tensor PCA, we show that an SNR ratio of λ ≳ pr1/2 is sufficient for accurate inference of any
linear functional of the signal tensor, where the SNR λ is defined as the smallest singular value across
all mode-j matricizations of T , denoted by λ = minj=1,2,3 σmin(Matj(T )).
3

[PDF PAGE 4]
Inference for Low-T ucker-Rank Linear F unctionals In this context, we achieve computationally
optimal results w.r.t. p: a sample size requirement of n ≳ p3/2r in tensor regression with sample
splitting and an SNR requirement of λ ≳ p3/4r1/2 in tensor PCA. These results are derived under relaxed
incoherence conditions of O(r1/2p−1/4), significantly less restrictive than the conventional incoherence
conditions of O(r1/2p−1/2) commonly assumed in the matrix and tensor inference literature [11, 47, 1, 32].
Furthermore, under stricter incoherence conditions of O(r1/2p−1/2) and assuming homogeneous singular
values, we establish that a statistically optimal sample size of n ≳ pr2 w.r.t. p in tensor regression is
sufficient to ensure valid inference.
Methodological Innovations Our framework differs from leave-one-out approaches that establish
entrywise confidence intervals using eigenvector distributions and ℓ2,∞ perturbation analysis [11, 47, 32,
1]. Instead, we employ a refined ℓ2 perturbation analysis of the spectral representation of perturbation
terms. Building upon the spectral representation of singular space estimation proposed by Xia [45],
our approach leverages the first-order perturbation term for inference while demonstrating that higher-
order terms are negligible. The assumption of a low Tucker rank for the loading tensor A simplifies the
complexity of perturbations in the ℓ2 norm, thereby achieving computational optimality.
Additionally, we develop novel concentration inequalities for quadratic and higher-order sub-Gaussian
polynomials, essential for bounding negligible terms in our analysis. The random components in the
estimated linear forms are expressed as polynomials of sub-Gaussian entries from noise or design tensors.
The sub-Gaussian design differentiates our approach from tensor completion settings, which typically
assume uniform sampling. Moreover, we relax the dependence on the condition number, ensuring that
our analysis remains computationally optimal even as the condition number κ of the signal tensor T
grows at a rate of O(p1/4) and becomes arbitrarily large.

plored. Using double-sample splitting, we demonstrate that asymptotic normality can be achieved with
a computationally optimal sample size of n ≍ p3/2r, w.r.t. p,

asymptotic normality.
1.2 Related work
Statistical inference for tensor regression remains a largely unexplored area of research. However, insights
can be drawn from methodologies developed for matrix and tensor completion under uniform sampling.
Most work in low-rank matrix completion focuses on entrywise inference, typically requiring incoherence
conditions. For example, Xia and Yuan [47] employed spectral perturbation techniques, while Chen et al.
[11] used a leave-one-out approach for entrywise statistical inference in matrix completion. Both methods
rely on incoherence conditions for the singular spaces Uj, imposing an upper bound on the ℓ2,∞ norm
of Uj to ensure uniform noise magnitudes across all rows. A comprehensive discussion of ℓ2,∞ geometry
can be found in Cape et al. [7]. However, verifying incoherence conditions in practical applications is
often non-trivial. The standard assumption ∥Uj∥2,∞ ≍
p
r/p represents the most stringent scenario,
achieving the lower bound of the ℓ2,∞ norm.
In the realm of tensor completion, Ma and Xia [32] recently tackled the inference problem for general
linear functionals of the signal tensor, extending the matrix completion framework of Xia and Yuan [47]
to tensors. However, their approach depends on the incoherence conditions of Agterberg and Zhang [1]
and requires that ∥A∥ℓ1 /∥A∥F remains bounded, thereby enforcing sparsity on the loading tensor A.
This sparsity aligns with Task 1 scenarios, where the linear functional ⟨A, T ⟩involves only a few tensor
entries.
Significant progress in tensor inference has been made by Xia et al. [49], who developed methods for
singular space inference for low-Tucker-rank tensors and entrywise inference for rank-one tensors under
Gaussian noise in tensor PCA. Building on this work, Agterberg and Zhang [1] extended entrywise
inference to low-Tucker-rank signal tensors with heteroskedastic sub-Gaussian noise in the tensor PCA
setting, achieving computationally optimal SNR under the incoherence condition ∥Uj∥2,∞ ≍
p
r/p for
each mode j.
While entrywise inference has been extensively explored in tensor completion and PCA models (47,
32, 1) as well as in matrix completion models (11, 47), inference involving linear functionals with many
non-zero entries, as described in Tasks 2 and 3, remains underdeveloped. In such cases, the ℓ1 norm of
A becomes substantial, rendering existing methods unsuitable.
A crucial component of our framework is obtaining an accurate initial estimate of the signal tensor
in tensor regression. Prior research has proposed various methods for low-rank tensor estimation in
regression settings [9, 35, 56, 39]. More recently, Zhang et al. [54] introduced an importance sketching
framework for tensor estimation under Gaussian designs, achieving minimax optimal error rates. Han
et al. [22] further extended non-convex optimization approaches to more general settings, demonstrating
minimax optimal rates for low-Tucker-rank tensors under both Gaussian and sub-Gaussian noise. These
advancements in tensor estimation provide the foundation for our inference methodology.
For both tensor regression and tensor PCA, our framework relies on accurately estimating the singular
spaces of each tensor mode. In tensor PCA, Zhang and Xia [53] established the minimax optimal rate for
estimating the singular spaces of a parameter tensorT ∈Rp1×p2×p3 in tensor PCA, under the assumption
of i.i.d. sub-Gaussian noise. These rates serve as critical benchmarks for our approach.

[PDF PAGE 6]
1.3 Organization
The remainder of this paper is organized as follows. In Section 2, we define the notation and terminology
used throughout. Section 3 introduces a comprehensive inference framework for tensor regression, pre-
senting algorithms for constructing debiased estimators both without sample splitting and with sample
splitting, along with their theoretical guarantees for asymptotic normality. This section also explores
the relationship between incoherence and alignment conditions and constructs confidence intervals with
proven theoretical properties, demonstrating their minimax optimality. We then extend our framework
to tensor PCA in Section 4, detailing the corresponding algorithms, theoretical guarantees, and the
construction of confidence intervals, while also establishing their minimax optimality in this context. A
proof sketch of the main theorems is presented in Section A. Numerical experiments are provided in
Section 5 to validate our methods, followed by a discussion of future work in Section 6. Technical proofs
are provided in the Supplementary Material.
2 Notation
Throughout this paper, we use the following notation. Tensors are denoted by calligraphic letters, such
as T , G, and A. Matrices are represented by uppercase letters like A and B, while vectors are indicated
by lowercase letters such as u and v. For two sequences of real numbers {an} and {bn}, write an ≲ bn
(resp. an ≳ bn) if there exist a constant C independent of n such that an ≤ Cb n (resp. an ≥ Cb n), and
write an ≍ bn if there are positive constants c and C such that c ≤ an/bn ≤ C for all n.
For a matrix A ∈ Rp1×p2 , we denote its projection matrix onto the left singular space by PA and
its projection matrix onto the right singular space by PA⊤ . The singular value decomposition (SVD) of
A is expressed as A = U1ΛU ⊤
2 , where Λ ∈ Rr×r is a diagonal matrix containing the singular values of
A, and U1 ∈ Rp1×r, U2 ∈ Rp2×r are unitary matrices. From this, PA = U1U ⊤
1 and PA⊤ = U2U ⊤
2 . Let
Λ = diag(σ1(A), σ2(A), ..., σr(A)), where the singular values are arranged in descending orderσmax(A) :=
σ1(A) ≥ σ2(A) ≥ · · · ≥σmin(A) := σr(A) ≥ 0. For any orthonormal matrix U ∈ Rp×r, we denote its
orthogonal complement by U⊥ ∈ Rp×(p−r).
This paper focuses on the analysis of 3-mode tensors. For such tensors, the Tucker rank of a tensor
T , denoted as rank( T ), is defined as a tuple ( r1, r2, r3) representing the ranks along each mode. Let
Vec(·) be the vectorization of matrices and tensors. The Frobenius norm of a tensor or matrix is given by
∥T ∥F = ∥Vec(T )∥2, while the spectral norm of a matrix is denoted by ∥A∥ = σ1(A). The inner product
of two tensor, A, T , is defined as ⟨T , A⟩ = Vec(T )⊤Vec(A). The vectorized ℓp-norm of a tensor or matrix
is written as ∥T ∥ℓp = ∥Vec(T )∥p, where, in particular, ∥T ∥ℓ∞ represents the maximum absolute value
among all entries.
For a tensor T ∈ Rp1×p2×p3 with Tucker rank ( r1, r2, r3), we define p = max {p1, p2, p3} and r =
max{r1, r2, r3} to simplify expressions involving the largest dimension or rank. The operator ×j denotes
the mode- j product between a tensor and a matrix. Specifically, for a tensor G ∈Rr1×r2×r3 and a
matrix V1 ∈ Rp1×r1 , the mode-1 product is defined as [ G ×1 V1]i1,i2,i3 = Pr1
j1=1[G]j1,i2,i3 [V1]i1,j1 for
i1 ≤ p1, i2 ≤ r2, i3 ≤ r3.
The set M(r1,r2,r3) of tensors with a fixed Tucker rank r = ( r1, r2, r3) forms a smooth embedded
submanifold of Rn1×n2×n3 , with dimension of r1r2r3 + P3
j=1 rj(pj − rj). For matrices in Rp×r, the
Stiefel manifold, denoted by Op×r is the set of matrices with orthonormal columns, i.e. Op×r = {M ∈
Rp×r | M ⊤M = Ir}.
We use Mat j(·) to denote the mode- j matricization (unfolding) of a tensor, which rearranges the
tensor into a matrix by stacking its mode- j fibers as columns. For a tensor T ∈Rp1×p2×p3 , the mode- j
matricization Matj(T ) ∈ Rpj ×pj+2pj+1 is defined as [Matj(T )]kj ,kj+1+pj+1(kj+2−1) = Tk1,k2,k3 , where j + 1
and j + 2 are computed modulo 3. For simplicity, we denote the mode- j matricization of a tensor T by
Tj := Matj(T ). This notation is used consistently throughout the paper.
For a tensor T with Tucker ranks (r1, · · ·, rm), we define its signal strength of T as
λ := λmin(T ) = min
j
{σrj (Matj(T ))}, (5)
which represents the smallest positive singular value among all tensor matricizations. Similarly, the
maximum signal strength is defined as
λ := λmax(T ) = max
j
{σ1(Matj(T ))}. (6)
6

[PDF PAGE 7]
The condition number of T , reflecting the ratio of the maximum to minimum signal strength, is given
by κ := κ(T ) = λ/λ.
3 Inference for Tensor Regression
3.1 Problem Setting
Consider a collection of i.i.d. random samples ( Xi, Yi)n
i=1 modeled as
Yi = ⟨T , Xi⟩ + ξi, (7)
where T is a low-rank tensor with Tucker rank ( r1, r2, r3), capturing the relationship between the scalar
responses {Yi}n
i=1 and the tensor covariates {Xi}n
i=1. The terms {ξi}n
i=1 represent independent noise.
Our objective is to perform statistical inference on the linear functional ⟨T , A⟩, where A ∈Rp1×p2×p3
is a prespecified loading tensor. In many practical scenarios, the loading tensor A may also have a low
Tucker rank, denoted by ( R1, R2, R3). For instance, entrywise inference corresponds to a special case
where A has a Tucker rank of (1 , 1, 1). The low-rank structures of the signal tensor T and the loading
tensor A will be leveraged to construct efficient estimators.
Our model, referred to as scalar-on-tensor regression, relates a multiway predictor to a scalar response.
This framework has broad applications across various fields, such as predicting clinical outcomes or
attributes from medical images [16, 56, 38]. While parameter estimation in tensor regression has been
extensively studied [56, 9, 35], statistical inference in this context remains largely underexplored.
We formalize the assumptions on the signal tensor T and the loading tensor A as follows.
Assumption 1 (Structures). (i). The signal tensor T follows a Tucker low-rank structure and can be
expressed as
T = G ×1 U1 ×2 U2 ×3 U3 ∈ Rp1×p2×p3 ,
where G ∈Rr1×r2×r3 is the core tensor, and Uj ∈ Opj ×rj is the factor matrix for j = 1, 2, 3-th mode of
T .
(ii). The loading tensor A can be represented as
A = B ×1 V1 ×2 V2 ×3 V3 ∈ Rp1×p2×p3 ,
where B ∈RR1×R2×R3 is the core tensor, and Vj ∈ Opj ×Rj is the factor matrix for j = 1, 2, 3-th mode of
A.
Remark 1. In this paper, we assume that the signal tensor T has a fixed or slowly growing Tucker
rank (r1, r2, r3). In contrast, the Tucker rank of the loading tensor A, denoted by (R1, R2, R3), may
vary across different scenarios. Notably, we consider the case where A has a full Tucker rank, i.e.,
(R1, R2, R3) = (p1, p2, p3).
Throughout our analysis, we assume that both the design tensors and the noise terms are sub-
Gaussian. Specifically, we impose the following assumptions.
Assumption 2 (Subgaussian design tensor) . In the tensor regression model (7), the design tensors
{Xi}n
i=1 are i.i.d. copies of a random tensor X , whose entries [X ]j1,j2,j3 are i.i.d., mean-zero, and σ-
subgaussian. Specifically, for each entry, we have ∥[X ]j1,j2,j3 ∥ψ2 ≤ σ, where σ is a positive constant, ∥·∥ψ2
denotes the Orcliz ψ2-norm, and there exist positive constants c and C such that cσ2 ≤ Var(Xj1,j2,j3 ) ≤
Cσ 2.
Assumption 3 (Subgaussian noise) . In the tensor regression model (7), the noise terms {ξi}n
i=1 are
i.i.d., mean-zero, and σξ-subgaussian. Specifically, we have ∥ξi∥ψ2 ≤ σξ, where σξ is a positive constant.
We begin by addressing the inference of general linear functionals.
3.2 Debiased Estimator of Linear Functionals without Sample Splitting
To efficiently estimate ⟨T , A⟩, we propose a multi-step algorithm leveraging an initial estimator.

Assumption 4 (Error bound for the initial signal tensor estimate). The initial estimate bT init is assumed
to have the same Tucker rank (r1, r2, r3) as the true signal tensor T . Additionally, the estimation error
satisfies ∥ bT init − T ∥F ≤ ∆, where ∆ = o(1) with probability at least 1 − P(E∆), and the event E∆ is

In Theorem 3.1, we analyze how this initial error affects convergence rates for asymptotic normality. This
optimal bound for the initial estimate ensures the nearly computational optimal sample size n ≳ p2r for
general linear functional inference. Using sample splitting, as shown in Theorem 3.2, can remove the
dependence between the initial estimator and the bias correction, and allow ∆ = o[(r log(p))−1] to suffice
for valid statistical inference.

Remark 4. The projection step is critical for both algorithmic performance and theoretical guarantees. A
candidate pivot quantity, bZ (1) = (nσ2)−1 Pn
i=1 ξiXi, is introduced after bias correction. However, directly
using ⟨A, bZ (1)⟩ results in suboptimal confidence interval lengths, as bZ (1) resides in the high-dimensional
space Rp1×p2×p3 rather than the low-Tucker-rank manifold M(r1,r2,r3). To address this, we project bZ (1)
onto the estimated singular spaces via P bUj
, restricting it to the low-Tucker-rank manifold. Since low-
Tucker-rank manifolds lack certain structural properties, the tangent space TT M(r1,r2,r3) at the signal
tensor T on M(r1,r2,r3) serves as a first-order approximation. Confidence intervals are constructed by
projecting the candidate pivot quantity bZ (1) onto the tangent space at the true parameter tensor T within
the manifold. Then, the projection in the tangent space PTT M(r1 ,r2 ,r3 ) ( bZ (1)) is the pivot quantity for
constructing the confidence interval.
3.3 Asymptotic Normality of Estimated Linear Functionals without Sample
Splitting
In this section, we establish the asymptotic normality of the estimator ⟨A, bT ⟩obtained from Section 3.2.
Assumption 6 (Sample size requirement) . The sample size n for tensor regression satisfies n ≥
C max{κ2p/λ2, pr}, where p = max {p1, p2, p3}, r = max {r1, r2, r3}, C is a constant depending on the
noise scales σξ and σ as defined in Assumption 3, and κ = λ/λ is the condition number of the signal
tensor T , λ, λ are defined in (5) and (6), respectively. To simplify the presentation of our results, we
assume p ≳ r2 throughout this paper.
Remark 5. This assumption ensures two key requirements. (i) Spectral Representation Validity: The
condition n ≳ κ2p/λ2, supports the spectral representation necessary for asymptotic normality, as in
Theorem 1 of Xia [45]. (ii) Degrees of Freedom: The sample size must scale with the tensor’s degrees of
freedom, r1r2r3 + P3
j=1 pjrj, which is statistically optimal for inference. Notably, larger singular values
(λ) reduce the sample size requirement.
With these assumptions, we proceed to establish the asymptotic normality of ⟨A, bT ⟩.

The theorem asserts that the estimator ⟨ bT , A⟩ is asymptotically normal, centered at the true lin-
ear functional ⟨T , A⟩, and scaled by the variance term ( σξ/σ)sA. The variance component sA re-
flects the variability introduced by projecting both the loading tensor A and the noise tensor bZ (1) =
(nσ2)−1 Pn
i=1 ξiXi onto the tangent space of the low-Tucker-rank manifold M(r1,r2,r3) at the true tensor
T . The asymptotic normal term ⟨PTT Mr (A), PTT Mr ( bZ (1))⟩, as introduced in Remark 4, represents the
Riemann metric on the tangent space TT between the loading tensor A and the candidate pivot quantity
bZ (1), defined in (8). This projection leverages a first-order approximation of bT around T within the
manifold, facilitating the normal approximation.
Remark 6. The error terms Ω1, Ω2, Ω3, and Ω4 represent various sources of negligible errors. Specifi-
cally, Ω1 accounts for negligible errors introduced by the artificial noise bZ (1) during bias correction, Ω2
captures errors arising from projection onto the estimated singular spaces, Ω3 provides a common upper
bound for errors from both bias correction and projection onto the estimated singular spaces. Among
these, Ω4, introduced by the initialization error in bias correction, typically dominates the negligible
terms. The dependence between the initial estimate and the bias-correction complicates the analysis and
requires a stricter sample size to ensure asymptotic normality.
Theorem 3.1 establishes conditions for valid inference of the linear functional ⟨A, T ⟩, even when the
loading tensor A has full Tucker rank. These results are summarized in the following corollary.
Corollary 3.1 (Asymptotic normality of estimated general linear functionals) . Under the conditions of
Theorem 3.1, assume the initial estimate is minimax optimal, i.e., ∆ ≍
p
pr/n. Further, suppose the
sample size satisfies n ≥ C1 max{κ2p/λ2, p2r}. Additionally, assume the following alignment condition
holds
sA ≥ C2 max
n
rp−1/2λ−1∥A ×j Uj∥F, rp−1λ−2∥A∥F
o
, (10)
where C1 and C2 are two constants depending only on the noise scales σξ and σ. Then, for any loading
tensor A ∈Rp1×p2×p3 , the estimator satisfies √n(⟨ bT , A⟩ − ⟨T, A⟩)/(σξ/σ · sA)
d
→ N(0, 1).
The minimax optimal initial estimation error ∆ and the sample size requirementn ≳ max{p2r, κ2p/λ2}
are crucial for mitigating the leading error term caused by the dependence between the initial estimate
and the bias-correction step. Minimax optimal initial estimators can be achieved using methods such as
projected gradient descent [22] or sketching [54] within the tensor regression framework.
For general linear functionals where the loading tensor A has full Tucker rank, no additional inco-
herence conditions are needed if the sample size is sufficiently large. The alignment condition ensures
that the variance component sA is sufficiently large, which is facilitated by a strong signal tensor T with
a larger minimum singular value λ. A stronger signal leads to a more favorable alignment condition.
Further discussion on the interplay between incoherence and alignment conditions is provided in Section
3.6.
The sub-Gaussian design tensor assumption (Assumption 2) aligns with the sub-Gaussian sampling
framework often used in compressed sensing [8]. Notably, our results demonstrate that under sub-
Gaussian designs, valid inference for general linear functionals is achievable without requiring the sample
size to scale with the total number of entries ( p1p2p3) in the parameter tensor T , provided the initial
10

[PDF PAGE 11]
estimates are accurate. This highlights the data compression benefits of low-Tucker-rank modeling, in
contrast to low-rank matrix trace regression where n ≳ p1p2 is typically required for inference with a
coefficient matrix M ∈ Rp1×p2 [5].
3.4 Debiased Estimator of Linear Functionals with Sample Splitting
In Theorem 3.1 and Corollary 3.1, we established that the general linear functional⟨A, T ⟩can be inferred
with a sample size of n ≳ p2, assuming r ≍ 1. While this guarantees valid inference, the required sample
size falls short of the computationally optimal rate n ≳ p3/2. This limitation arises from reusing data
for both bias correction and initial estimation, which constrains the convergence rate.
In tensor regression, there exists a gap between the computationally optimal sample size n ≳ p3/2,
as highlighted in Remark 4.3 of Han et al. [22], and the lower bound of the sample size determined
by the degrees of freedom for a tensor with Tucker rank-( r1, r2, r3), given by n ≳ r1r2r3 + P3
j=1 pjrj.
This discrepancy raises the natural question: Can the statistically optimal sample size be achieved while
ensuring valid inference?
In the following section, we address this affirmatively by employing a sample-splitting strategy. This
approach eliminates the dependence between the initial estimation and the debiasing step. Specifically,
the observed data is divided into two disjoint subsets: dataset I and dataset II. One subset is used
for initial estimation, and the other for debiasing, ensuring that the debiasing process is independent of
the data used for obtaining initial estimates. The detailed algorithm for tensor regression with sample
splitting is provided below.
Step 1: Initialization
Using the first dataset I := {Y (I)
i1 , X(I)
i1 }n1
i1=1, apply the initial estimation procedure to obtain the initial
(typically biased) tensor estimate bT init,(I) ∈ Rp1×p2×p3 and initial estimates of the factor matricesbU init,(I)
1 ,
bU init,(I)
2 , bU init,(I)
3 . Similarly, using the second dataset, II := {Y (II)
i2 , X(II)
i2 }n2
i2=1, apply the same procedure
to obtain the tensor estimate bT init,(II) ∈ Rp1×p2×p3 and factor matrices bU init,(II)

from the other dataset, effectively eliminating the dependence between the initial estimates and the bias
correction.

sample sizes under appropriate conditions.

then a sample size of n ≥ C max{κ2p/λ2, p3/2r log(p)3/2} is sufficient for valid inference, where C, C2 and

as n, p → ∞.
Remark 7 (Role of λ). So far, our discussion of computational and statistical optimality has focused on
the case λ ≍ 1. In the tensor regression setting, our analysis reveals that increasing the signal strength
λ relaxes the requirements for incoherence and alignment conditions necessary for valid inference. For
example, if λ ≳ p1/4 and r ≍ 1, κ≍ 1, a sample size n ≳ p3/2 suffices for low-rank linear functional
inference without requiring an incoherence condition. If λ ≳ p1/2 and r ≍ 1, κ≍ 1, even sample size
n ≳ p is sufficient for valid inference, again with no incoherence condition needed. However, the role
of λ in determining estimation quality and the computationally/statistically optimal sample size (lower
bounds) in the tensor regression framework is underexplored in the existing literature. Similar results have
been reported in [54]. In practice, researchers often have limited prior knowledge of the signal strength
of T , highlighting a gap that warrants further investigation.
13

[PDF PAGE 14]
3.6 The Incoherence and Alignment Conditions: A Geometric Interpreta-
tion on the Low-Tucker-Rank Manifold
To clarify the roles of the incoherence and alignment conditions in our framework, we analyze them from
the perspective of low-Tucker-rank manifold geometry. Let M(r1,r2,r3) denote the manifold of tensors
T ∈Rp1×p2×p3 with Tucker rank (r1, r2, r3). At a point T = G ×1 U1 ×2 U2 ×3 U3, the tangent space is
parameterized as
TT M(r1,r2,r3) =
n
eG ×1 U1 ×2 U2 ×3 U3 +
3X
j=1
G ×j eUj ×j+1 Uj+1 ×j+2 Uj+2
 eU ⊤
j Uj = 0
o
,
where eG ∈Rr1×r2×r3 and eUj ∈ Rpj ×rj are free parameters that represent perturbations to the core
tensor and factor subspaces, respectively. The orthogonal projection of a tensor A ∈Rp1×p2×p3 onto the
tangent space TT M(r1,r2,r3) is defined as
PTT M(r1 ,r2 ,r3 ) : Rp1×p2×p3 → TT M(r1,r2,r3),
A 7→ A ×1 PU1 ×2 PU2 ×3 PU3 +
3X
j=1
Mat−1

PUj⊥ AjP(Uj+2⊗Uj+1)G⊤


,
where PUj is the projection matrix onto the subspace spanned by Uj, PUj⊥ = I − PUj projects onto the
orthogonal complement of Uj, and Mat −1
j is the inverse of the mode- j matricization operator.
The alignment conditions are commonly assumed in matrix inference [47, 11] and tensor inference
[1, 32]. These alignment conditions ((10), (11), and (12)) ensure that the loading tensor A is sufficiently
aligned with the tangent space of the low-Tucker-rank manifold at T . Specifically, the alignment condi-
tions guarantee that the magnitude of the asymptotic normal term⟨PTT M(r1 ,r2 ,r3 ) (A), PTT M(r1 ,r2 ,r3 ) ( bZ (1))⟩
dominates the perturbation terms in the normal space.
The tangent space TT M(r1, r2, r3) can be decomposed into a direct sum of the following subspaces:
TT M(r1,r2,r3) = TG ⊕ T1 ⊕ T2 ⊕ T3,
where
TG =
 eG ×1 U1 ×2 U2 ×3 U3 | eG ∈Rr1×r2×r3
	
,
captures perturbations to the core tensor, and
Tj =

G ×j eUj ×j+1 Uj+1 ×j+2 Uj+2 | eU ⊤
j Uj = 0, eUj ∈ Rpj ×rj
	
,
for j = 1, 2, 3, captures perturbations to the factor matrices in each mode. This decomposition facilitates
the analysis of how the loading tensor A interacts with different components of the tangent space. A
similar characterization of perturbation sources on the tangent space is also employed in the proof of the
minimax lower bound in Theorem 3.4.
In addition to perturbations in the normal space, the error terms Ω 1 and Ω 3 in the theorems
also include perturbations in the direction of A ×1 PU1 ×2 PU2 ×3 PU3 , which lie within the tangent
space—specifically in the subspace TG. These perturbations are not controlled by the alignment con-
dition and constitute leading terms in higher-order perturbations. To address this, the incoherence
condition ((1) and (4)) requires that the projection of A onto TG is relatively small compared to its
projection onto the entire tangent space. For each mode j = 1 , 2, 3, the condition imposes an upper
bound on
∥PUj AjP(Uj+2⊗Uj+1)G⊤
j
∥F
∥PUj⊥ AjP(Uj+2⊗Uj+1)G⊤
j
∥F
or ∥PUj Aj(PUj+2 ⊗ PUj+1 )∥F
∥PUj⊥ Aj(PUj+2 ⊗ PUj+1 )∥F
,
where the condition limits the influence of A in the direction of core tensor perturbations. This in-
coherence condition is satisfied when ∥U ⊤
j Vj∥, representing the angle between the mode- j subspace of
T = G ×1 U1 ×2 U2 ×3 U3 and A = B ×1 V1 ×2 V2 ×3 V3, is sufficiently small for j = 1, 2, 3. This implies
that the loading tensor A is not fully aligned with the subspace TG of the tangent space, which represents
perturbations in the core tensor. Since the core tensor subspace is a component of the tangent space,
the incoherence condition and the alignment condition are, in some sense, inherently at odds with one
another.
To illustrate this more clearly, consider the entrywise inference as an example. The combination of the
incoherence and alignment conditions assumed in prior works [11, 32, 47] requires maxj ∥Uj∥2,∞ ≍
p
r/p,
14

[PDF PAGE 15]
effectively enforcing that ∥Uj∥2,∞ reaches its lower bound. This imposes highly restrictive constraints
on the factor matrices, limiting these methods to scenarios where the factor matrices exhibit a high level
of incoherence.
In contrast, our framework relaxes these stringent requirements, enhancing the flexibility and ap-
plicability of inferential procedures in low-rank tensor settings. Specifically, by not requiring the in-
coherence condition, our normal approximation framework for general linear functionals, presented in
Section 3.3, offers greater flexibility while achieving nearly computationally optimal sample size require-
ments. Furthermore, when combined with sample splitting, our framework attains computationally
optimal sample sizes for low-rank linear functionals under a weaker incoherence condition (1), where
maxj ∥Uj∥2,∞ ≲ r1/2p−1/4, compared to those in the existing literature.
Additionally, our results show that increasing the signal strength λ of the underlying parameter
tensor allows for even weaker incoherence and alignment conditions. Consequently, our tensor regression
framework, along with the inferential procedures for tensor PCA introduced subsequently, extends the
applicability and flexibility of statistical inference in low-rank tensor models.

3.8 Minimax Optimality of the Confidence Interval Length
A natural question is how the proposed inferential procedures compare to other methods. To evaluate
their performance, we analyze the length of the confidence intervals constructed in our framework and
compare them with the minimax lower bound for the tensor regression setting. In this section, we
establish that these confidence intervals achieve minimax rate optimality, demonstrating the efficiency
of our approach.
Our analysis builds upon results from related literature [4] but diverges from the Cram´ er–Rao lower
bound traditionally studied in information geometry [37, 32]. Unlike the Cram´ er–Rao framework, which
focuses on parameter estimation under unbiasedness constraints, our work characterizes the optimal
performance of confidence intervals under general perturbations in the tangent space of the low-Tucker-
rank manifold. This perspective allows us to rigorously show that the proposed procedures attain the
fundamental limits of inference accuracy in tensor regression.
In what follows, the parameter space Θ( λ, κ) is defined as
Θ(λ, κ) :=

where λ represents any nonzero singular value of Mat j(T ) for each mode j = 1, 2, 3. We also define the
class of confidence intervals with coverage level 100(1 − α)% as

4 Inference for Tensor PCA
4.1 Problem Setting
In this section, we focus on the tensor Principal Component Analysis (PCA) model, defined as
Y = T + Z, (20)
16

[PDF PAGE 17]
where Y ∈Rp1×p2×p3 is the observed tensor, T is the underlying signal tensor, and Z is a noise tensor.
The signal tensor T admits a low Tucker-rank decompositionT = G ×1U1×2U2×3U3, where Uj ∈ Opj ×rj .
The goal is to perform valid statistical inference on the linear functional ⟨T , A⟩, where A ∈Rp1×p2×p3
is a prespecified loading tensor. We also consider cases where A is a potentially low-rank tensor with
Tucker rank (R1, R2, R3), expressed as A = B ×1 V1 ×2 V2 ×3 V3. Entrywise inference is a special case
where the loading tensor has Tucker rank (1 , 1, 1). Structural assumptions on the signal tensor T and
the loading tensor A are detailed in Assumption 1.
In our analysis, we assume that the noise tensor Z has i.i.d. sub-Gaussian entries, formalized as
follows.
Assumption 7 (Sub-Gaussian Noise in tensor PCA) . The noise tensor Z is entrywise i.i.d. with mean
zero and sub-Gaussian, and its ψ2 Orlicz norm is bounded by σ >0, i.e., ∥[Z]j1,j2,j3 ∥ψ2 ≤ σ.
This implies that the variance of each entry satisfies Var([ Z]j1,j2,j3 ) = E([Z]2
j1,j2,j3 ) ≤ Cσ 2 for some
constant C >0.
4.2 Estimation of Linear Functionals
To estimate ⟨T , A⟩ under the tensor PCA setting, we propose the following algorithm.
Step 1: Initialization.
We use the observed tensor Y as initial estimate for the signal tensor T , and obtain initial estimates of
the loading factors bU init

shorthand, we denote bU (0)

debiasing step is required in tensor PCA.
Assumption 8 (Error Bound for Initial Estimates of Singular Spaces) . We assume that the initial
singular space estimates satisfy the minimax-optimal error bound ∥ bU (0)

initialization without loss of generality.
Step 2: Two-step Power Iteration.
Using the initial estimates bU (0)

In the special case of entrywise inference under the tensor PCA setting, our approach imposes sig-
nificantly weaker incoherence and alignment conditions compared to Agterberg and Zhang [1], which
assumes ∥Uj∥2,∞ ≍
p
r/p and sA ≳ r2p−5/4∥A∥F for loading tensor A of the form ei ⊗ ek ⊗ el. Our
incoherence and alignment conditions align with those in Theorem 7 of Xia et al. [49], which focused
18

[PDF PAGE 19]
on entrywise inference for rank-one signal tensors in tensor PCA. However, our results generalize this
to tensors of any low Tucker rank. Additionally, unlike existing literature [1, 32, 47], our framework re-
mains computationally optimal even when the condition number κ diverges at a rate of O(p1/4). Beyond
entrywise inference, our method naturally extends to other low-Tucker-rank linear functionals, offering
broad applicability.
In the tensor completion setting, Ma and Xia [32] demonstrated that statistically optimal sample
sizes and SNRs are sufficient for accurate inference using ℓ2,∞ perturbation analysis. The statistically
optimal SNR is achievable because the incoherence condition significantly simplifies uniform sampling.
However, the ℓ2,∞ perturbation analysis in Ma and Xia [32] cannot be readily extended to sub-Gaussian
settings, especially when the loading tensor A is not sparse, as required by the condition ∥A∥ℓ1 /∥A∥F
being bounded in Ma and Xia [32]. Furthermore, a consistent initialization via HOSVD De Lathauwer et al.
[12] requires computationally optimal size (Remark 1, Zhang and Xia [53]). Consequently, it remains
unclear whether the statistically optimal SNR is attainable for inference under tensor PCA settings.
In addition to inference for low-Tucker-rank linear functionals, Theorem 4.1 implies the following
asymptotic normality for estimated general linear functionals without requiring incoherence conditions.
Corollary 4.2 (Asymptotic normality of estimated general linear functionals). Suppose that the loading
tensor A has rank at most (p1, p2, p3). When λ ≥ C max{κp1/2, pr1/2}, and sA defined in (2) satisfies
the alignment condition

j=1,2,3


, (23)
where C and C2 are positive constants depending only on the noise scale σ, then it holds that (⟨ bT , A⟩ −
⟨T , A⟩)/(σ · sA)
d
→ N(0, 1).

data is denoted by D = Y. The following theorem establishes the minimax lower bound on the expected
length of confidence intervals, for estimating linear functionals of the signal tensor T under the tensor
PCA model.

Theorem 4.3 demonstrates that the confidence intervals derived from our estimators, which achieve
this lower bound, are minimax optimal. In the context of entrywise inference under the tensor PCA
setting, Agterberg and Zhang [1] provides a similar minimax lower bound with a differently defined
parameter space. This result highlights the effectiveness of our inference procedure for constructing
confidence intervals for linear functionals of low-rank tensors in high-dimensional settings.
5 Numerical Experiments
In this section, we conduct numerical simulations to validate the proposed central limit theorems for
estimated linear functionals. We consider three settings: tensor regression without sample splitting
(Theorem 3.1), tensor regression with sample splitting (Theorem 3.2), and tensor PCA (Theorem 4.1).
Our simulations are designed to assess the performance of our inference framework under various
scenarios. We begin by generating a core tensor G ∈R3×3×3 with full Tucker rank. The diagonal entries
Gj,j,j are sampled uniformly from the interval [ λ, λ], where λ = κλ while the off-diagonal entries are
set to be zero. Tensor operations are implemented in R using the rTensor package [25]. To construct
the singular subspaces, we consider both coherent and incoherent settings. Coherent singular subspaces
Uj are generated by performing SVD on matrices where the first contains only a large value equals √p,
while the remaining entries are sampled from a standard normal distribution. In contrast, incoherent
singular subspaces are generated by applying SVD to random Gaussian matrices with i.i.d. standard
normal entries. Using these subspaces, the signal tensor T is constructed as T = G ×1 U1 ×2 U2 ×3 U3.
We set p1 = p2 = p3 = p.
We consider three scenarios for the loading tensor A. In the first scenario with a full-Tucker-rank
loading tensor, A is generated with entries drawn independently from a standard normal distribution
and then rescaled to have unit Frobenius norm. This ensures that A has full Tucker rank ( p1, p2, p3)
with probability 1. In the second case, we use a low-Tucker-rank loading tensor defined as
A = 1q
⌊2p1/4
1 ⌋⌊2p1/4
2 ⌋⌊2p1/4
3 ⌋

X⌊2p1/4

ej1 ⊗ ej2 ⊗ ej3 ,
which has a Tucker rank of (1 , 1, 1) and maintains unit Frobenius norm. This low-rank construction
presents a significant challenge for existing frameworks, such as Ma and Xia [32], because it leads to
∥A∥ℓ1 /∥A∥F ≳ p1/4, thereby violating their specified condition. To ensure compliance with the inco-
herence conditions in (1) and (4), the singular subspaces are generated to be incoherent ( ∥Uj∥2,∞ ≲
r1/2p−1/2) with high probability. In the third scenario, we perform an entrywise inference using these
incoherent singular spaces to validate the proposed central limit theorem with a statistically optimal
sample size in the context of tensor regression.

In the tensor regression setting, the observation noise {ξi}n

The signal strength and condition number of the signal tensor T are controlled by setting λ = 1 and
λ = κλ. For the inference of general linear functionals and low-Tucker-rank linear functionals, we set
κ = √p and κ = p1/4 respectively, reflecting a more relaxed condition number compared to previous
work [47]. For entrywise inference, we set κ = 1 to achieve the statistically optimal sample size.

5.2 Simulation under the Tensor PCA Setting
In the tensor PCA setting, the noise tensor Z is generated with i.i.d. standard normal entries, satisfying
σ = 1. The observed tensor is constructed as Y = T +Z, and the initial singular subspaces bU init
j ∈ Rpj ×rj

For general linear functional inference, the signal strengthλ is varied asλ ∈ {p1/2r1/2, p3/4r1/2, pr1/2},

strength λ is varied as λ ∈ {pr3/4, pr1/2, p5/4r1/2}, also with κ = p1/4. Figure 2 illustrates the nor-
mal approximations achieved under varying signal strengths and condition numbers for both inference
scenarios.

setting for entrywise inference in tensor PCA. The signal tensor is defined as T = u1 ⊗ u2 ⊗ u3, where

6 Discussion
In this work, we present a unified framework for statistical inference on general linear functionals of signal
tensors in both tensor regression and tensor PCA settings. Our approach nearly achieves computation-
ally optimal sample size or signal-to-noise ratio (SNR) requirements for general linear functionals and
precisely meets these optimal requirements for low-rank linear functionals, paving the way for further
advancements in tensor-based statistical inference.
While our analysis focuses on sub-Gaussian noise, future research could explore the robustness of our
framework under heavy-tailed distributions or Huber contamination models to broaden its applicability.
Robust estimation techniques for matrix and tensor parameters have been studied in areas such as low-
rank matrix recovery [50], matrix completion [43], and tensor decomposition [36]. However, inference
under heavy-tailed noise remains relatively unexplored. Another promising direction for future work is
extending our framework to handle structured sparse loading tensors, as discussed in Zhang and Han
[52].
Practical implementation of our framework on large-scale datasets necessitates efficient computation
and storage of projection matrices, alongside iterative updates in high-dimensional settings. Future
studies might focus on algorithmic enhancements, such as randomized or distributed approaches, to
handle large tensors while reducing computational and memory demands. For instance, sketching al-
gorithms have been explored in tensor estimation problems, including tensor regression [54] and tensor
PCA [33]. However, the application of sketching techniques for statistical inference remains an open area
for exploration.
While the discussion primarily focuses on scenarios with i.i.d. sub-Gaussian noise in tensor PCA
or design tensors in tensor regression, the framework can be extended to handle heteroskedastic sub-
Gaussian noise, as shown in Agterberg and Zhang [1] for tensor PCA. This extension holds if the entrywise
noise of the design tensor in tensor regression or the observational noise in tensor PCA, denoted by
σj1,j2,j3 , satisfies σ ≤ σj1,j2,j3 ≤ σ.
Assume the vectorized design tensor, Vec(Xi) ∈ Rp1p2p3 , is a sub-Gaussian random vector with mean
zero and covariance matrix Σ ∈ Rp1p2p3×p1p2p3 . The debiased initial estimator is then expressed as

This appendix is structured into two main parts, providing supplementary details and proofs sup-
porting the main text.
The first part contains the technical proofs of the main theorems. We start with a proof sketch of the
main theorems in Section A. While the proofs for tensor regression and tensor PCA share similar ideas,
the regression case is considerably more complex. The rest of the appendix is structured as follows: in
Section B and Section C, we prove the asymptotic normality of the estimated linear functional ⟨ bT , A⟩
under the setting of tensor regression without or with sample splitting, corresponding to Theorem 3.1 and
Theorem 3.2, respectively. We also prove the asymptotic normality of the estimated linear functional
⟨ bT , A⟩ under the setting of tensor PCA, corresponding to Theorem 4.1, which closely parallels the
technical results in the regression case.
We consider scenarios where variance components of the signal tensor and noise are estimated from
observed data. The asymptotic normality of the estimated linear functional with these estimated vari-
ances is discussed in Section K, specifically for tensor regression in Section K.1 and for tensor PCA in
Section K.2.
Furthermore, Section L establishes the minimax optimality of the confidence interval length under
tensor regression (see Section L.1) and tensor PCA (see Section L.2).
The second part of the appendix adds more details to the main text. Several examples of confidence
intervals for inferring low-Tucker-rank linear functionals are provided in Section M. In addition, we
summarize the algorithms of estimating the underlying linear functional⟨A, T ⟩. Specifically, the inference
procedures for tensor regression (with and without sample splitting) and tensor PCA are summarized in
Section N and Section N.2.
A Proof Sketch of Main Theorems
In this section, we outline the proof of the main theorem for tensor regression without sample splitting
(Theorem 3.1). The proof of asymptotic normality under the sample splitting setting, with adjustments
for data partitioning follows a similar framework. Notably, sample splitting simplifies the analysis by
eliminating the dependence between the initial estimation and the bias correction.
The proof of Theorem 4.1 in Section 4, which establishes the asymptotic normality of the estimated
linear functional ⟨ bT , A⟩ in the tensor PCA setting, also aligns closely with the tensor regression frame-
work. However, a key distinction is that tensor PCA does not require an initial estimate, setting it apart
from tensor regression.
Our objective is to analyze the asymptotic distribution of the linear functional ⟨ bT − T, A⟩, which can
be decomposed as:

In the following subsections, we established upper bounds for perturbation terms of varying orders in
the spectral representation under the setting of tensor regression with sample splitting. In particular, we
will show that the first-order perturbation term is the leading term. Throughout this section, we assume
that ∆ ≥ (σξ/σ)
p
p/n, which implies that the initial estimate satisfies the minimax lower bound as well.
Different from the scenario without sample splitting, sample splitting removes the dependency between
the initial estimate and debiasing procedure. As a result, the higher-order pertubation terms will vanish
at a faster rate.
E Preliminary Upper Bounds for Tensor Regression with Sam-
ple splitting
This section contains the essential lemmas for the proof of Theorem 3.2. Although the following contents
are similar to the lemmas for the proof of Theorem 3.1, the double sample-splitting largely reduces the
upper bound of negligible terms by removing the dependence between the projection and bias-correction
using two separate datasets. As the sample-splitting divides the original dataset to two subsets, by
symmetry, we only consider one subset.

I Concentration Inequalities for Tensor PCA

∥A ⊗ B∥2,∞ ≤ ∥A∥2,∞ · ∥B∥2,∞ . (336)

K Proof of Asymptotic Normality with Plug-in Estimates

PCA (Theorem 4.2) with plug-in estimates, respectively.

M Specific Examples
Our framework is versatile and applicable to inferring any linear functional of the signal tensor T . In
this section, we demonstrate its utility through three specific scenarios: entrywise inference, comparing
tensor entries, and inference of the average of entries along a mode. All confidence intervals discussed
below are valid under the conditions specified in Corollary 3.2 for tensor regression or Corollary 4.1 for
tensor PCA. For the inference of general (full-rank) linear functionals, the conditions for valid inference
are provided in Corollary 3.1 for tensor regression and Corollary 4.2 for tensor PCA.
M.1 Entrywise inference
Entrywise inference aims to provide statistical statements about individual entries of the signal tensor
T . This task has been extensively studied in matrix PCA [11, 32, 17] and recently extended to tensor
PCA [49, 1].
To perform entrywise inference for a specific tensor entry Tk1,k2,k3 , we consider the linear functional
⟨A, T ⟩= ⟨ek1 ⊗ ek2 ⊗ ek3 , T ⟩. Applying Theorem 3.3 and Theorem 4.2, we construct the following
confidence intervals in tensor regression,
cCI

setting, the entrywise confidence interval is of the same form with ( bσξ/bσ) · bsA ·

M.2 Inference for the Row/Column Mean
Researchers often aim to understand the average effect across one mode of the signal tensor. For instance,
in collaborative filtering, this could involve inferring the average rating an item receives among all users
[19, 34]. In addition, spatial patterns of gene regulation can be identified by averaging gene expression
measurements over all time points [29].
To infer the average effect across the first mode, consider the loading tensor A = 1

N Summary of Inference Procedure
N.1 Inference procedure for tensor regression
We first outlines the steps to estimate the linear functional ⟨A, T ⟩for tensor regression when the entire
dataset is used jointly without splitting. The summarized procedure is detailed in Algorithm 1. Fur-
thermore, with sample splitting, the procedure summarized in Algorithm 2, addresses the dependency
between debiasing and estimation by partitioning the dataset into two independent subsets. Each subset
is used to complement the estimation in the other.
N.2 Inference procedure for tensor PCA
The inference procedure for tensor PCA is designed to estimate the linear functional ⟨A, T ⟩when the
signal tensor is observed with additive noise. Algorithm 3 summarizes the key steps.
89

[PDF PAGE 116]
Algorithm 1 Inference Procedure for Tensor Regression without Sample splitting
1: Initialization
2: Obtain initial estimates bT init ∈ Rp1×p2×p3 and factor matrices bU (0)
j := bU init
j for j = 1, 2, 3.
3: Debiasing
4: Compute the debiased tensor: bT unbs = bT init + 1
nσ2
Pn
i=1

Yi − ⟨bT init, Xi⟩

